  Two new files:
  - `production/deployment.md` (~35 min reading, 402 lines). Four production deployment shapes for stateful agents: Shape 1 (FastAPI + Docker + PostgreSQL + Redis + vector DB — the default for ≤1M conversations/month, modeled after Wassim El Bakkouri's April 2026 production template + the Use Apify March 2026 Postgres-checkpointing recipe); Shape 2 (durable execution layered under the agent — LangGraph checkpointing + Temporal/Inngest/Hatchet/Cloudflare Workflows, anchored in the March 23, 2026 OpenAI Agents SDK + Temporal Python SDK GA integration); Shape 3 (serverless — Lambda/Cloudflare Workers/Modal — with explicit when-it-fits and when-it-doesn't framing including cold-start mitigation); Shape 4 (on-prem + air-gapped — vLLM/Ollama/TGI with the EU AI Act August 2026 enforcement deadline driver and the 5-10-developer single-node concurrency ceiling). Decision tree distinguishes shapes by volume + workflow wall-clock + side-effect shape, with compliance constraint overriding everything. Includes the stateful-vs-stateless agent design dimension across all four shapes. Closes with five operational-discipline practices (cost attribution on day one, three-layer rate limiting, kill switches, connection pool sizing, pinned Docker images) — the things teams shipping their first agent typically learn through a 3am page. 16 dated 2026 references including Cordum, Zylos Research, AgentMarketCap, DigitalApplied, TrueFoundry, FutureAGI, agentgateway, Hivenet, Ranjan Kumar, Use Apify, El Bakkouri's template, the LangChain runtime guide, and Thomas Cherickal's local-LLM survey.

The pre-launch checklist organized into eight layers: secrets and credentials (8 items including per-service keys + key rotation rehearsal + JWT signing-key rotation independent of API keys); rate limiting and quota management (7 items including three-layer per-user/per-tenant/per-LLM-provider pattern + token-aware limits + per-conversation cost-budget cap); kill switches and feature flags (7 items including per-agent, per-tenant, global; the four-line test for kill-switch operability; high-risk action confirmation gates per Wiz February 2026); rollback and deployment safety (8 items including 5-minute container rollback target + eval gates in CI/CD + automatic rollback on quality regression + the 4pm-Tuesday-prompt-change failure mode from FutureAGI February 2026); observability and alerting (9 items including per-request cost attribution with four token layers + rate-of-change alerts on quality metrics + 30-day-hot + 90-day-cold log retention); abuse detection and refusal monitoring (7 items including prompt-injection attempt detection + PII leakage detection on outputs + per-tool-per-agent rate monitoring + audit log with cryptographic chain integrity); runbook readiness (8 items including the kill-switch runbook + cost-spike investigation + misrouted-conversation debug + who-to-page matrix); the dress rehearsal (8 items closing with the 30-minute pre-flight call). 8 dated 2026 references.

  **The "bookend modules first" sequencing rationale**: per the Path 07 scaffold's own framing, Modules 1 + 8 are the most actionable starting point for any team with a deployment deadline. Module 1 answers "how do I structure the deployment" — the four-shapes decision tree (FastAPI+Postgres / Temporal-layered / serverless / on-prem) — and Module 8 answers "what do I check before flipping production traffic on" — the eight-layer checklist. Together they make the path useful from Day 1 even with Modules 2-7 still in queue.

  **The Module 1 four-shapes framing as the load-bearing decision tree**: the 2026 production literature has consolidated around four deployment shapes that fit different volume × workflow-wall-clock × compliance combinations. Most teams start on Shape 1 (FastAPI + Postgres + LangGraph checkpointing), add Shape 2 (Temporal layer) when workflows cross the 30-minute or cross-service threshold (the March 23, 2026 OpenAI Agents SDK + Temporal GA integration removed most of the friction here), graduate to Shape 3 (serverless) opportunistically for specific bursty pieces, and reach Shape 4 (on-prem) only when compliance forces it. The decision tree IS the production decision, not a survey of options.

  **The Module 8 eight-layer organization as the production discipline pattern**: each layer captures a distinct failure mode the 2026 production literature identifies as recurring. Layer 1 (secrets) prevents credential-leak post-mortems; Layer 2 (rate limiting) prevents quota exhaustion ("drained the API quota in 20 minutes" per TrueFoundry May 2026); Layer 3 (kill switches) prevents runaway-agent damage; Layer 4 (rollback) prevents the 4pm-Tuesday-prompt-change incident; Layer 5 (observability) prevents debugging blind; Layer 6 (abuse detection) prevents user-side-attack damage; Layer 7 (runbook) prevents 3am-debugging-from-scratch; Layer 8 (dress rehearsal) catches the gaps the other seven miss. The discipline is exhaustive by design — teams that skip layers learn them through incidents.

  **Cross-references back to Path 03 are deliberate**: Path 03 v2 patterns (cost budgeting, retries, escalation) and v3 projects (the deployment shape illustrations) ARE the substrate Path 07 builds on. The Path 07 scaffold's prerequisite framing made this explicit; the shipped Module 1 and Module 8 honor it with concrete cross-links to Pattern 3 (escalation tiers used in alerting), Pattern 4 (per-agent cost budgeting extended to per-conversation budget caps), Pattern 5 (retries used in multi-LLM-provider fallback), and the three Path 03 v3 projects as deployment-shape examples.

  Selection-guide-not-vendor-ranking framing. The "Best fit" column distinguishes by dominant constraint (control / role-decomposition / OpenAI-locked / Anthropic-native / type-safety / A2A-first / Microsoft-stack / data-layer / conversational); the comparison table doesn't order frameworks; the decision guide names a single applicability rule per framework. Multi-framework deployments across A2A boundaries are the increasingly-common 2026 production shape — the decision becomes "which framework per agent" rather than "which framework for the whole system."

  **The "selection guide, not vendor ranking" framing as the editorial discipline**: nine frameworks each get their own "Best fit" row and their own "Choose X if" decision rule, with no ordering. The reason matters — by mid-2026, six of the nine frameworks are vendor-native first-party (OpenAI Agents SDK, Claude Agent SDK, Google ADK 1.0, Microsoft Agent Framework 1.0, Pydantic AI from the Pydantic team, AutoGen-now-MAF from Microsoft), and a "best framework" claim across the set would mean "best vendor" — which is not a question that has an answer outside a specific organization's stack commitments. The Path 06 evaluation-frameworks-deep-dive shipped with the same editorial discipline; this page extends it.

  **The A2A cross-framework escape hatch as the production reality** (per Project 3 and [Linux Foundation April 9, 2026 A2A 150-org milestone](https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/a2a-protocol-surpasses-150-organizations-lands-in-major-cloud-platforms-and-sees-enterprise-production-use-in-first-year-302737641.html)): cross-organization production deployments increasingly run agents from different frameworks (Org A LangGraph supervisor → Org B CrewAI specialist → Org C MAF compliance review) without either framework needing to know about the other. The decision guide treats this as the load-bearing pattern; the migration-paths section's last entry ("Any framework → A2A cross-framework") makes this explicit. The framework-choice decision is therefore *per agent* in cross-org deployments, not per system.

  **The model-diversity acceptance criterion as defense against the coherence trap**: Project 2's faithfulness judge must use a different model family (e.g., GPT-4o-mini or Gemini 1.5 Flash) than its Claude Sonnet specialists. Per Zylos Research May 2026's formalization of Huang et al. ICLR 2024, iterative self-critique amplifies confidence without adding information when the generator and evaluator share error modes. This is the load-bearing structural defense — without it, the judge approves the specialists' hallucinations because it shares their blind spots. The acceptance rubric makes this criterion #1 and asserts on the model strings in CI.

Most production cross-org multi-agent deployments in early 2026 deferred the security story as "ship later." Project 3 explicitly does not — OAuth 2.1, JWS-RS256 signature verification, AP2 mandates, append-only audit log are M1-M6 build milestones, not extensions.

  Three new pattern pages:
  - `patterns/02-router.md` (~10 min). One-of-N selection: a thin classifier picks exactly one downstream handler per request; each handler is its own Pattern 01 instance with a focused toolset. Earns its place when distinct task *types* live in the same product but want different tools — the Pattern 01 break-even point at ~10 tools is the structural argument. Production sweet spot is 3-5 categories per BSWEN March 2026; beyond ~6 categories accuracy collapses and a hierarchical structure (Pattern 04) becomes the right answer. Code shape uses Pydantic structured-output routing decisions with `Literal[...]` enum + `confidence` + `rationale`; downstream handlers receive original request unchanged. The "reserve the bigger model for execution; use a fast model for classification" production lever framed explicitly: router calls should run 10-15% of total request cost when sized correctly (Haiku-class), bumping to Opus-class adds 40-60% for marginal accuracy. Five 2026 source citations: BSWEN March 2026 (LangGraph `Command(goto=...)` shape), Gurusup April 2026 (orchestrator-with-routing as ~70% of production multi-agent deployments; 50-100ms embedding vs 1-2s LLM classifier latency), Huzaifaali October 2025 (full LangGraph implementation), Botpress 2025 (LLM-routing vs legacy classifier comparison), NVIDIA AI Blueprints llm-router (production routing applied to model selection).

  - `patterns/08-agentic-rag.md` (~12 min). The agent decides whether to retrieve, what to retrieve, when to stop — retrieval as a tool, not a fixed pre-step. Five sub-patterns (query rewrite + hybrid retrieval + reranking + iterative retrieval + self-check) catalogued per FutureAGI 2026; production agentic RAG uses 3-4 of them, rarely all 5. Explicit cost-curve framing: 3-10× tokens, 2-5× latency vs one-pass RAG per MarsDevs April 2026; the trade is reliability on hard questions for cost on easy ones; well-tuned systems route easy through 1 retrieve, hard through 4. The "naive RAG fails at retrieval ~40% of the time" baseline per Lushbinary 2026 is the structural argument for the pattern. Code shape framework-free with `MAX_RETRIEVAL_HOPS = 5` cap, faithfulness judge gating the final answer (not per retrieval), judge feedback loops back into the agent loop. When NOT to use named explicitly: single-fact lookups, retrieval surface already 85%+ recall@k, faithfulness can't be measured (the coherence-trap reference back to Pattern 07), corpus changes mid-conversation. Production framework consensus per MarsDevs 2026: LangGraph + LlamaIndex Workflows + Ragas + Phoenix + Langfuse. VentureBeat Q1 2026 RAG tracker data: hybrid retrieval adoption tripled 10.3%→33.3% Jan→March 2026.

  - `patterns/09-deep-research.md` (~12 min). The deep-research-agent shape that ChatGPT Deep Research, Claude Deep Research, Gemini Deep Research, and Perplexity Pro all instantiate. Composes Patterns 03 + 06 + 07 + 08 into a single application class: lead agent decomposes into sub-questions, dispatches parallel specialists (each running Pattern 08 internally), aggregates findings, coverage judge gates the final report with replan loop on gaps. Production benchmarks per Microsoft DRACO (Zhong et al., arXiv:2602.11685, February 2026): Accuracy, Completeness, Objectivity across 100 complex research tasks spanning 10 domains; Microsoft's Researcher with Critique uses Council mode (Anthropic + OpenAI in parallel + judge synthesizes agreements/divergences). Cost-economics framing explicit and substantial: 50× to 250× single Q&A tokens; 5-30 minute wall-clock typical, hours for Gemini Deep Research Max; production deployments route between Pattern 08 (cheap path) and Pattern 09 (expensive path) via Pattern 02 by question shape. Five real-world implementations catalogued: OpenAI Deep Research (Feb 2025 launch; o3 variant + MCP integration Feb 2026), Claude Deep Research (200K context + verified citations), Gemini Deep Research Max (extended-iteration variant), Perplexity Deep Research (hybrid model selection), Microsoft Researcher (Council mode). Open-source canonical: ByteDance DeerFlow.

  **The 2026 cost-economics framing Pattern 09 makes explicit**: deep-research agent runs consume 50× to 250× the tokens of a single Q&A. Production deployments measure question shape upfront (sentence count, ambiguity, time-sensitivity) and route between Pattern 08 (1-pass to 4-hop agentic RAG, $0.10-$0.50 per question) and Pattern 09 (decomposed deep research, $5-$25 per question) via a Pattern 02 router. The router IS the cost-control gate. Without it, an under-specified question triggering max replans + max sub-questions × max hops creates the 250× cost tail that dominates infrastructure bills within weeks.

  **The Pattern 02 production lever Pattern 02 makes explicit**: routing classifier costs are typically 10-15% of total request cost when sized correctly (Haiku-class for the classification call, Sonnet/Opus for downstream execution). Inverting that — Opus-class for classification — adds 40-60% to total cost for marginal accuracy improvement per BSWEN March 2026 production analysis. This is the single biggest cost lever in production routing systems and the most common antipattern (teams reach for the most accurate model for classification because the routing decision feels important; the math says use the cheapest model that hits the accuracy threshold).

  Three new pattern pages:
  - `patterns/04-hierarchical-teams.md` (~11 min). Pattern 03 nested one level: multiple supervisors, each owning specialist workers, coordinated by a top-level supervisor. Earns its place past ~5-6 specialists when one supervisor's tool/agent surface gets too wide — restoring per-level tool count to a tractable size at each layer. Code shape: LangGraph's `create_supervisor(supervisors)` composition recipe — pass compiled team-supervisor graphs as if they were individual agents. Production-cost tradeoff explicit: 1.5-2× the token spend of flat Pattern 03 for the same task; routing accuracy near-100% per level at 3-4 specialists per team vs flat Pattern 03's 85-90% above 6 specialists. End-to-end reliability is the *product* of per-level reliability — 95% per level × two levels = 90% end-to-end. The most common production failure (per LifetidesHub 2026): top supervisor routing the same subtask repeatedly to the same team — set per-task ceiling at the top level (typical: 6-8 dispatches per request). Five real-world examples (Anthropic deep research, OpenAI Deep Research, Perplexity research modes, Microsoft Copilot for Enterprise, LangGraph's hierarchical tutorial). Anchors 2026 source material: CallSphere May 2026 (4-specialist per-team production threshold + max_iterations defaults), BetterLink May 2026 (supervisor-of-supervisors framing), LifetidesHub May 2026 (orchestration decision tree: 6+ workers → hierarchical), LangGraph supervisor README (2026 recommendation to tool-calling-pattern-directly for most use cases).

  - `patterns/05-swarm-handoff.md` (~11 min). Peer agents transfer control to each other directly with no top-level coordinator. The 2026 provenance reality call-out is explicit and substantial: OpenAI Swarm (October 2024) was experimental and was archived March 2025; the OpenAI Agents SDK (March 2025+) replaced it but per DigitalApplied March 2026 the SDK's handoffs "implement the supervisor pattern, not a peer swarm." True peer-to-peer hand-off mid-conversation is rarer in production than the marketing suggests. The pattern's defining tension named explicitly: most "swarm" deployments described in 2026 vendor material are structurally Pattern 03 with a triage entry-point. Code shape uses OpenAI Agents SDK 0.17+ (May 2026) — agents have `handoffs` lists naming the peers they can transfer to; Runner.run orchestrates; tracing is OpenTelemetry-compatible. Direct framing of observability cost: worst-of-any-multi-agent-pattern without dedicated tracing. Composes with Pattern 10 (HITL approval at hand-off boundaries for high-stakes transfers) and Pattern 12 (cross-process equivalent via A2A). Four 2026 source citations: DigitalApplied March-May 2026 (supervisor-vs-swarm distinction), Respan May 2026 (Agents SDK vs Swarm migration), Gurusup April 2026 (framework comparison), byteiota March 2026 (5-20× token cost range for multi-agent).

  - `patterns/07-reflection.md` (~12 min). Generator-critic-revise loop until critic approves or hop cap reached. The defining tension named explicitly upfront: the Huang et al. ICLR 2024 result (LLMs cannot reliably self-correct reasoning errors using purely intrinsic capabilities) and the 2026 Zylos Research formalization of the "coherence trap" — when generator and evaluator share correlated error modes, iterative self-critique amplifies confidence without adding information. The pattern works when the critic has *external* signal: unit tests, retrieval grounding, schema validators, a different model. Reflexion (Shinn et al. NeurIPS 2023) baseline: 91% HumanEval pass@1 vs 80% GPT-4 single-pass — 11-point absolute gain. Code shape framework-free with `MAX_ITERATIONS=4` hop cap, separate generator and critic callables, drafts + critiques history accumulated for feedback-driven regeneration. Four variations covered (Self-Refine one-model; Reflexion verbal memory; LATS tree-search; Process Reward Models). Tradeoffs explicit: 2-4× single-pass latency + cost; same-model self-critic without external signal often *degrades* performance per Huang 2024. Composes with Pattern 01 (wraps the agent loop), Pattern 03 (a worker is itself a reflection loop), Pattern 06 (executor wrapping for per-step refinement), Pattern 10 (HITL fallback when hop cap reached). Six 2026 source citations including arxiv:2303.11366 (Reflexion), arxiv:2310.01798 (Huang), Anthropic Building Effective Agents (December 2024 "evaluator-optimizer" naming), Zylos Research March + May 2026 (production maturity arc, coherence trap formalization), Beancount April 2026 (WebShop failure deep-dive), MLPills January 2026 (breadth-vs-depth framing).

  **The hard 2026 provenance call that Pattern 05 makes**: OpenAI Swarm (the term most people use casually) was archived March 2025; the production successor (Agents SDK) implements supervisor-with-handoffs, NOT a peer swarm per DigitalApplied 2026's explicit framing. Most production "swarm-shaped" deployments described in 2026 vendor material are structurally Pattern 03 in disguise. The pattern page calls this out directly because the conceptual confusion is the most common reason teams pick the wrong pattern — they read about "swarm" in marketing material, build what's actually a supervisor-with-handoffs, then wonder why the architecture doesn't match the marketing description. Pattern 05's "When NOT to use" section's third bullet directly addresses this: "You're using 'swarm' as a synonym for 'multi-agent.'"

  **The hard 2026 provenance call that Pattern 07 makes**: pure intrinsic self-correction (same-model generator and critic, no external signal) does NOT reliably improve reasoning performance per Huang et al. ICLR 2024 — and the 2026 Zylos Research information-theoretic formalization explains why (correlated error modes between generator and critic produce a coherence trap, not a correction signal). The pattern works when the critic has external signal (unit tests, retrieval grounding, schema validators, diverse-model critics). The Reflexion 91% HumanEval result *uses* external unit-test signals — that's why it works. Page calls this out explicitly in three places (intent paragraph, "When NOT to use", tradeoffs table) to prevent the misread.

- **🎉 Path 04 closure — Module 7 (MCP + A2A composition) + Lab 30 (Batch 50).** **Closes Path 04 to 7/7 complete.** Path 04 is the second learning path to reach full module completion after Path 01. The path is now `~7 hours shipped` against the planned 6-10 total.

  Four new files:
  - `concepts/tools/mcp-a2a-composition.md` (~14 min, Module 7 concept page). The closer for Path 04. Covers the asymmetric composition shape (MCP for agent-to-tool internally, A2A for agent-to-agent externally — they layer, they don't compete); when composition is the right shape vs overkill (boundaries cross when team/process/organization boundaries cross); the composed-worker code shape (~30 lines on top of Lab 28: `MCPClient` opened inside `execute()`, two tool calls inside one MCP client lifetime, result packaged as A2A artifact); the orchestrator code shape via `A2ACardResolver` + `ClientFactory(config=ClientConfig(httpx_client=http, streaming=False))`; push notifications with atomic registration via `SendMessageConfiguration.task_push_notification_config` + `return_immediately=True` (the deferral from Module 6 closes here — the standalone `client.create_task_push_notification_config()` call races with task completion; the atomic path doesn't); the `X-A2A-Notification-Token` shared-secret pattern in webhook headers; idempotency requirements for webhook receivers (2-3 notifications fire per task: artifact + status transitions); signature verification wired into `ClientFactory.create_from_url` via the `signature_verifier` callback (the protobuf round-trip pattern from Module 6 plugs in). Closes with the four limits composition does NOT solve: capability inference (intent routing is application logic), native multi-hop tracing (`traceparent` propagation is manual), cross-protocol type system (the worker bridges JSON Schema and protobuf), cross-protocol backpressure (queues build up across the composition). Anchors the canonical "MCP for tools, A2A for agents" framing per a2a-mcp.org March 2026 (reports 40-60% workflow-velocity improvement for composed deployments).

Eight-step structure: environment → author MCP server (2 tools) → author composed worker (A2A executor opens MCPClient inside execute()) → author webhook receiver → spawn + health-probe three subprocesses → discover worker via A2ACardResolver → dispatch via ClientFactory + send_message → push notifications via atomic SendMessageConfiguration registration → wrap-up. Anti-scope: no OAuth2 token flow, no JWKS-based key distribution, no multi-worker orchestration, no distributed tracing across composition, no HMAC validation on webhook receiver, no real LLM calls, no DatabaseTaskStore.

  **The one composition gotcha surfaced and taught explicitly in Lab 30 Step 7**: push notification registration races with task completion if you use the standalone `client.create_task_push_notification_config(cfg)` call AFTER `send_message()` returns. With the synchronous default, `send_message` waits for the task to terminate before returning; by the time the standalone push-config call runs, the task is in terminal state and no notification will fire (the push sender fires on state *transitions*; if no transitions remain, nothing fires). The atomic pattern — wrapping the push config inside `SendMessageConfiguration.task_push_notification_config` with `return_immediately=True` — registers the config before the task starts and returns the SUBMITTED task without waiting. Step 7 demonstrates the atomic pattern; the lab cell output explicitly notes the race that the alternative pattern would hit. Multiple notifications per task (2-4 typically: initial Task + state transitions + artifact publish events) means webhook receivers MUST be idempotent (`(task_id, transition_index)` is the standard idempotency key).

  **Encapsulation principle made concrete**: Lab 30 Step 5 prints the worker's full Agent Card and verifies — programmatically — that the string "MCP" doesn't appear anywhere in the card and the internal MCP server URL (port 9991) isn't exposed. The teaching point: the card describes the worker's *capability* (the contract); the MCP server is the *implementation*. Orchestrators get the contract; they never get the implementation. The worker is free to swap its internal MCP server for direct database calls tomorrow without breaking the contract; the card doesn't change; the orchestrator doesn't notice.

Verified end-to-end: spawn `production_agent_server.py` as a subprocess; fetch the signed Agent Card from `.well-known/`; verify the JWS signature with the corresponding public key; demonstrate tamper rejection (both signature mutation and payload mutation); send two `SendMessage` requests with API-key auth; inspect the SQLite DB directly to confirm tasks are persisted; terminate + respawn the server; retrieve the persisted task across the restart via `GetTask`; stream a third message via `SendStreamingMessage` and observe the canonical 4-event SSE sequence (initial Task SUBMITTED → statusUpdate WORKING → artifactUpdate → statusUpdate COMPLETED); read the OTel spans file the server wrote (verified output during build: 133 spans across 20 unique names from 5 distinct trace IDs across Steps 4-7). One non-obvious production gotcha surfaced and taught explicitly — the SDK's `create_agent_card_routes()` injects three v0.3 backward-compat fields (`preferredTransport`, `protocolVersion`, `url`) into the served card JSON that aren't in the protobuf schema; to verify the JWS signature, the verifier must round-trip the fetched card through `ParseDict(..., ignore_unknown_fields=True)` + `MessageToDict()` to recover the canonical signed bytes. The JWS payload must be byte-identical to what was signed; transport-injected fields must be stripped on the verifier side.

  This batch grounds the security treatment in two complementary January 2026 arxiv papers: the [Zhou et al. paper (arxiv:2601.10955)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.10955) which the original Path 04 scaffold cited and which covers resource-amplification DoS attacks (658× token inflation), plus the newer [Maloyan paper (arxiv:2601.17549)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.17549) which covers protocol-level vulnerabilities (23-41% attack amplification). The two papers target different attack surfaces — economic exhaustion vs. confidentiality / integrity — but share a root cause: the MCP tool layer's text-visible fields are attacker-controllable and trusted by the LLM. Module 4 names both.

  Four new files:

  - `concepts/tools/mcp-security-threat-model.md` — Module 4 concept page (~14 min, intermediate, marked 🔴 fast-changing for the specific defenses while the threat model itself is stable). NEW. Covers: why MCP changes the security picture (tool descriptions as attack surface, multi-server amplification per Palo Alto Unit 42 with 5 servers → 78.3% attack success, the connect-time vs. runtime trust gap); the five MCP attack classes per TrueFoundry April 2026 taxonomy (tool poisoning, indirect prompt injection through tool responses, rug pulls, credential sprawl, audit blind spots) with concrete 2026 PoCs including Invariant Labs' whatsapp-mcp exfiltration and the official GitHub MCP PAT-theft attack; protocol-level vulnerabilities from arxiv:2601.17549 (847 attack scenarios across 5 servers, 23-41% amplification vs non-MCP, MCPSec extension reducing attack success 52.8% → 12.4%); server-lifecycle threats from arxiv:2503.23278 (Hou et al., four phases: creation/deployment/operation/maintenance); empirical state from arxiv:2506.13538 (1,899 servers studied, 43% with command-injection flaws); the six defense layers (server allowlists at the client, tool-description and tool-response sanitization, least-agency / least-privilege, human-in-the-loop at the risk gate, audit logging across the boundary, sandboxing and rate limits at the server); OWASP MCP Top 10 (beta, MCP01-MCP10 stable for citation); explicit acknowledgment that universal prompt-injection defenses don't exist, pattern-based scanning catches canonical patterns but not novel variants, fingerprint defenses catch description-change rug pulls but not runtime-condition variants. 17+ verified 2026 sources cited including arxiv papers; OWASP MCP Top 10 + OWASP Agentic Top 10; Practical DevSecOps March 2026 (30+ CVEs Jan-Feb 2026); TrueFoundry April 2026; MCP Playground February 2026; OpenclawMCP March 2026; Descope January 2026; WorkOS March 2026; Microsoft April 2025; Invariant Labs PoCs.

Step 1 writes `malicious_toy_server.py` simulating three attack patterns (tool poisoning via `add_numbers` with `<IMPORTANT>` directive, response injection via `search_documents` returning poisoned content for "meeting" queries, rug-pull via `safe_calculator` whose description rewrites based on `LAB27_RUG_PULL` env flag for reproducibility). Step 2 demonstrates Attack 1 against undefended client — shows the asymmetry between tool descriptions as the LLM sees them (full directive visible) vs. as a typical UI renders them (just the first sentence). Step 3 builds `description_scanner.py` with pattern-based detection (XML-tag directives, override imperatives, sensitive-filesystem references, env-var exfiltration patterns, persuasion patterns) and verifies it flags the poisoned tool. Step 4 demonstrates Attack 2 (response injection) undefended. Step 5 builds `sanitize_response()` with redact-and-wrap defense, demonstrates the LLM context now has explicit `<tool_response>...</tool_response>` delimiters plus suspicious patterns replaced with `[REDACTED-SUSPICIOUS-PATTERN]`. Step 6 demonstrates Attack 3 (rug-pull): first connection benign description, env flag flipped, second connection malicious description. Step 7 builds fingerprint cache + audit log: `sha256(description)` per `(server_id, tool_name)` stored in `fingerprint_cache.json`, append-only `audit_log.jsonl` with `event`, `server`, `tools`, `fingerprint_findings`, `timestamp`. Step 8 stretch: side-by-side defense effectiveness table; explicit demonstration of an evasive description (technical-jargon-encoded directive) that the regex scanner misses; explicit acknowledgment that pattern-based defenses raise attacker cost rather than preventing universal attacks. End-to-end execution verified via nbconvert with FastMCP 3.3.1; all 10 code cells executed cleanly without errors; no external API calls required.

  - `quizzes/foundations/mcp-security-threat-model.md` — NEW. 8 single-select questions (~10 min, mixed difficulty, pass 6/8). YAML front-matter per locked-quiz-format convention; full `<details>` reveals after each question. Coverage: Q1 (easy) tool poisoning vs. generic prompt injection; Q2 (easy) arxiv:2601.17549 23-41% amplification finding; Q3 (easy) multi-server compromise propagation per Palo Alto Unit 42 78.3% measurement; Q4 (medium) arxiv:2506.13538 43% command-injection finding and what it implies for defense prioritization; Q5 (medium) response injection through trusted servers (the official-GitHub-MCP-PAT-theft attack class); Q6 (medium) rug pulls and the limits of fingerprint defenses; Q7 (hard) defense-prioritization — server allowlists as highest single mitigation impact; Q8 (hard) pattern-based scanner evasion and the explicit limits of any single defense layer.

 Ships the implementation-level companion to Pattern 11 (MCP integration): the consuming-side concept page + a 90-110 min intermediate lab + an 8-question quiz. The Path 04 module table updates from 2/7 to 3/7 shipped modules; this is the third content batch for Path 04 (after Batches 42 scaffold and 43 Modules 1+2).   Five new files:

  - `concepts/tools/building-an-mcp-client.md` — Module 3 concept page (~13 min, intermediate). NEW. Covers: what an MCP client actually does (connect / discover / translate / defend); the minimum-viable client (~10 lines of FastMCP); the schema-translation layer with MCP → Anthropic and MCP → OpenAI conversions; tool-name collision handling via server-name prefixes; multi-server orchestration with `MultiServerMcpClient`; connection-lifetime tradeoffs (long-lived vs per-request); discovery patterns (config file → MCP Registry at `registry.modelcontextprotocol.io` → MCP Server Cards at `/.well-known/mcp-server-card.json`); the five production failure modes (schema drift, tool-call timeout, auth-token expiry, server unavailable, response payload too large) each with concrete defensive code; FastMCP 3.1's code-mode escape hatch for token bloat (15K → 2-3K tokens per Apigene April 2026). 2026 grounding: 4M+ daily FastMCP downloads; 27,432+ aggregated servers per MCPfinder May 2026; 70%+ Python MCP servers use FastMCP per tech-insider April 2026; Cloudflare's "Code Mode" 98%+ token savings per WorkOS March 2026; `server.json` standard per Glama October 2025; enterprise MCP gateway patterns per TrueFoundry / Kong / agentic-community.

  - `labs/26-mcp-client-from-scratch/README.md` — Lab brief (90-110 min, intermediate). NEW. Three-layer architecture (single-server → multi-server → defensive) + agent integration. Nine-step structure: environment setup → minimal client against Lab 25's server → second toy server (time_server) → MultiServerMcpClient with collision-free routing → DefensiveMultiServerClient with three of five defenses wired (timeout + circuit-breaker + response-size limit) → schema-translation layer → Pattern 01 agent loop integration with real Anthropic API call → token-bloat measurement → stretch directions. Explicit anti-scope: not server authoring (Lab 25's territory beyond the trivial time_server); not OAuth 2.1 client flows (Path 07); not MCP Registry integration (future module); not OpenTelemetry observability (Path 06); not A2A; not MCP security threat model (Module 4).

  - `labs/26-mcp-client-from-scratch/lab.ipynb` — NEW. 21-cell notebook (11 markdown + 10 code; 34 KB; stripped — 0 outputs, 0 execution counts). Step 0 environment setup + FastMCP version check + optional Anthropic SDK check. Step 1 writes a trimmed notes-server copy and exercises all primitives with `client.list_tools()` / `list_resources()` / `list_resource_templates()` / `call_tool()` / `read_resource()`. Step 2 writes a second toy `time_server.py` with `get_current_time` + `get_status` (the latter deliberately colliding with a tool we add to the notes server in Step 3). Step 3 builds the `MultiServerMcpClient` class with server-name prefixes + collision-free routing — demonstrates calling `notes__get_status` vs `time__get_status` to expose the routing fix. Step 4 builds `DefensiveMultiServerClient` extending it with `CircuitState` tracking + `asyncio.wait_for` per-tool timeout + response-size truncation. Step 5 demonstrates the schema-translation layer (MCP `Tool` → Anthropic tools format) with `mcp_tool_to_anthropic()`. Step 6 wires the client into a real Pattern 01 agent loop with `anthropic.Anthropic().messages.create(model="claude-haiku-4-5", tools=tool_schemas, messages=...)` — skips gracefully if `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` isn't set. Step 7 token-cost measurement (1 server vs 2 server scope; extrapolation to 5×10 = ~6K+ tokens; FastMCP 3.1 code-mode 2-3K target). Step 8 stretch directions. All code cells use top-level `await` (no `asyncio.run` — Jupyter incompatibility lesson from Batch 43).

  - `quizzes/foundations/mcp-client-and-discovery.md` — NEW. 8 single-select questions (~10 min, mixed difficulty, pass 6/8). YAML front-matter per locked-quiz-format convention; full `<details>` reveals after each question with explanations + back-pointer to the source concept-page section. Coverage: Q1 tool-name collisions across multi-server clients + server-prefix fix (easy); Q2 Pattern 01 / MCP loop symmetry (easy); Q3 per-tool timeout defense (easy); Q4 FastMCP 3.1 code-mode for token bloat (medium); Q5 OAuth token refresh + retry-once pattern (medium); Q6 schema-drift defense with TTL cache + push invalidation (medium); Q7 MCP Registry discovery architecture for fleet deployments (hard); Q8 circuit-breaker defense for failing server in multi-server context (hard).

  Three new pattern pages (all follow the `patterns/README.md` 8-section format):

  - `patterns/03-supervisor-workers.md` — NEW. ~12 min, stable. Architecture-level companion to Path 03 v1 Module 1; implemented in Lab 10 and Lab 14. Mermaid flowchart shows supervisor → N specialist workers → aggregation. Implementation sketch uses Pydantic-typed WorkerTask + WorkerResult — references Path 03 v2 Pattern 1 (handoff contracts) for the full six-field schema. Anchors on Anthropic's *Building Effective Agents* (Dec 2024) orchestrator-workers framing; digitalapplied April 2026 ("hierarchical wins over swarm in production almost every time"); CrewAI / AutoGen / LangGraph framework-native expressions. Cross-references Lab 10, Lab 13, Lab 14, `concepts/multi-agent/supervisor-worker-pattern.md`. Tradeoffs table covers the 2-4× token cost vs Pattern 01 and the supervisor-drift failure mode.

  - `patterns/06-plan-and-execute.md` — NEW. ~12 min, stable. Architecture-level companion to Path 03 v1 Module 3; implemented in Lab 12 and Lab 15. Mermaid flowchart shows Planner → Plan → Executor → Decide → Re-planner loop. Implementation sketch uses Pydantic-typed PlanStep + StepResult with explicit `depends_on` field for DAG-based parallel execution. 2026 grounding: LangChain LLMCompiler's 3.6× speedup measurement; dev.to March 2026's 92% accuracy / 85% ReAct / 3000-4500 vs 2000-3000 token measurements; Medium May 2026's "planning failure propagation" framing; SAP October 2025 P-t-E security analysis (the responsible-agentic-AI compliance framing); arxiv [Architecting Resilient LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2509.08646) security advantages. Cross-references Lab 12, Lab 15, `concepts/multi-agent/{plan-and-execute,planner-executor-pattern}.md`. Implementation sketch uses different planner-model (reasoning-heavy) and executor-model (cheaper) — the architectural separation that makes the pattern economical.

  - `patterns/10-human-in-the-loop.md` — NEW. ~13 min, stable. Architecture-level pattern for compliance and safety. Mermaid flowchart shows agent action → 4-dimension risk gate → review queue → human reviewer → approve/modify/deny/escalate/timeout → audit log. The four risk dimensions (irreversibility, blast radius, compliance exposure, confidence) drive routing. Implementation sketch uses Pydantic-typed ActionRisk + ApprovalDecision with explicit RiskPolicy thresholds. 2026 compliance grounding: EU AI Act Article 14 (August 2, 2026 deadline for high-risk systems); California SB-833 (July 1, 2026); NIST AI RMF; Galileo April 2026; myengineeringpath.dev March 2026 (4-dimension framework); Anna Jey April 2026 (review-the-decision-not-the-run); Strata.io May 2026 (challenge-and-response approval; automation-bias mitigation); Prefactor April 2026 (risk-based policy engine); Elementum AI March 2026 (compliance-as-architectural-feature). Real-world examples cover financial-trading agents, customer-support, code-generation agents (Claude Code / Cursor / Codex CLI), healthcare diagnostic agents, LangGraph's `interrupt()` primitive.

Total: 5 new files + 5 modifications, 10 files in the batch, all internal links resolve.

  Five new files:

  - `concepts/tools/mcp-foundations.md` — Module 1 concept page (~14 min, intermediate). NEW. Covers: the N×M-to-N+M integration-cost framing that justifies MCP; the three primitives (tools / resources / prompts) and the "who initiates" axis (LLM / host / user) that distinguishes them; the three-layer architecture (host application / MCP client / MCP server) with bidirectional sampling and elicitation arrows; the JSON-RPC 2.0 wire format with concrete `tools/list` and `tools/call` examples; the two transports (stdio for local desktop integrations / Streamable HTTP as the production default since the 2026 spec); the five-phase session lifecycle (initialize → capability advertisement → invocation → optional reverse calls → shutdown) with a Mermaid sequence diagram; OAuth 2.1 + token-scoping conventions; what MCP is *not* (not A2A, not an LLM API wrapper, not a function-calling format); where the protocol works and where it strains (long-running tools, streaming output, cross-server versioning). 2026 grounding: dev.to April 2026 (97M monthly SDK downloads, 81,000 GitHub stars by March 2026); truthifi.com May 2026 (10,000+ active public servers under Linux Foundation governance); dev.to March 2026 (the MCP-vs-A2A protocol-separation framing); a2a-mcp.org March 2026 (MCP 2026 roadmap including Server Cards); arxiv:2601.10955 (the resource-amplification threat model previewed for Module 4).

  - `concepts/tools/building-an-mcp-server.md` — Module 2 concept page (~13 min, intermediate). NEW. Covers: why FastMCP exists (the raw SDK's ~80-line boilerplate becomes ~30 lines with FastMCP); the 30-line server walkthrough with three tools; the three FastMCP decorators (`@mcp.tool` / `@mcp.resource` / `@mcp.prompt`) and the primitives they map to; the type-hint-to-JSON-Schema inference table (str/int/float/bool/list/dict/Literal/Optional/Pydantic BaseModel); how docstrings become tool descriptions and parameter descriptions; the MCP Inspector workflow with a three-step development loop (write → run `mcp dev inspector` → debug via JSON-RPC log); the Python client side via `fastmcp.Client` async context manager; the deployment-pattern split between stdio (local) and Streamable HTTP (production) with TLS, OAuth 2.1, persistence, observability, rate-limiting; three common mistakes (vague tool descriptions / confusing tools with resources / mutating state without idempotency keys). 2026 grounding: gofastmcp.com (FastMCP powers ~70% of MCP servers across all languages; ~1M downloads/day); github.com/prefecthq/fastmcp (the project source); firecrawl.dev April 2026 (FastMCP 3.0 release Jan 19 2026; component versioning, granular authorization, OpenTelemetry instrumentation, multiple provider types); tech-insider.org April 2026 (the 12-step production tutorial); jangwook.net May 2026 (the under-30-line working example); blog.jztan.com February-April 2026 (the under-100-lines reference build); machinelearningmastery.com February 2026 (the minimum-viable server pattern).

  - `labs/25-mcp-server-from-scratch/README.md` — Lab brief (90-110 min, intermediate). NEW. Three-channel testing strategy (MCP Inspector visual / Python client programmatic / Streamable HTTP curl). Nine-step lab structure: environment setup → three tools → two resources → one prompt → Inspector workflow → Python client → four deliberately-broken-tool failure modes → Streamable HTTP with token auth → stretch (SQLite persistence, more CRUD, rate limits). Explicit anti-scope: not A2A (Module 5), not MCP security threat model (Module 4), not production OAuth flow tuning (Path 07), not MCP client-side beyond `fastmcp.Client` (Module 3), not Claude Desktop frontend integration. Five concrete failure modes the lab makes you observe (vague descriptions; schema-inference silent failures; resources-vs-tools confusion; auth-less Streamable HTTP = network-exposed dict; stateful sessions don't survive restarts).

  - `labs/25-mcp-server-from-scratch/lab.ipynb` — 21-cell notebook (11 markdown + 10 code; 30 KB; stripped — 0 outputs, 0 execution counts). NEW. Step 0 environment setup + FastMCP version check. Step 1 builds three tools and writes them to `notes_server.py`. Step 2 appends two resources (`notes://all` static + `notes://{title}` templated). Step 3 appends the prompt template + the `mcp.run()` main block. Step 4 documents the MCP Inspector workflow (subprocess launch commented out by default for re-runnable notebook hygiene). Step 5 exercises the server programmatically with `fastmcp.Client` in async context manager — list capabilities, call each tool, read a templated resource. Step 6 the four schema-inference failure modes walkthrough: vague-docstring vs descriptive-docstring (FastMCP `Client(server)` in-memory transport pattern for introspecting schemas without spawning subprocesses); `int` vs `Literal[1,2,3]` for constrained parameters. Step 7 writes the Streamable HTTP variant (`notes_server_http.py`) with `MCP_BEARER_TOKEN` environment variable + 127.0.0.1 bind for local-only execution. Step 8 stretch directions (SQLite, more CRUD, rate limits, Claude Desktop config). All code cells parse cleanly; output-stripped.

  - `quizzes/foundations/mcp-foundations-and-server.md` — 8 single-select questions (~10 min, mixed difficulty, pass 6/8). NEW. YAML front-matter per locked-quiz-format convention; full `<details>` reveals after each question with explanations + back-pointer to the source concept-page section. Coverage: Q1 N×M integration cost framing (easy); Q2 resource-vs-tool-vs-prompt classification (easy); Q3 stdio-vs-Streamable-HTTP transport choice (easy); Q4 the right decorator + the right type for a constrained MCP tool (medium); Q5 vague-docstring failure mode (medium); Q6 idempotency-key pattern for side-effectful tools (medium); Q7 catch-all-tool dilutes tool-selection signal (hard); Q8 prompt-vs-tool by who-initiates axis (hard).

 Extends the Batch 39 foundation trio (handoff contracts / shared-state boundaries / escalation and fallback) with the next operational trio (per-agent cost budgeting / retry policies / cross-agent provenance). The six-pattern set is what the 2026 production multi-agent literature is converging on; Batch 41 completes three of the four next-candidate patterns named after Batch 39 (the fourth, role-scope leakage detection, is deferred to Batch 42 as outside the smallest-useful scope).

  Markdown-only batch. No new labs · no new notebooks · no new dependencies · no `.lycheeignore` change. Three new pattern files + three documentation modifications (patterns README + Path 03 README + this CHANGELOG entry).

  - `learning-paths/03-multi-agent-systems/patterns/04-per-agent-cost-budgeting.md` — NEW. ~15-min pattern. Same eight-section structure as the Batch 39 patterns. The four budget dimensions per agent (token budget, tool-call ceiling, cost cap in USD, wall-clock budget) with explicit defaults by role (supervisor 50k/5/$0.50/30s; researcher 100k/8/$1.00/60s; writer 30k/0/$0.30/30s; critic 40k/1/$0.40/30s; executor 60k/3/$0.60/30s; calibrate from 90th-percentile traces after week one). Three exhaustion behaviors (hard-stop with partial-result return; hard-stop with Pattern 3 escalation; supervisor-approved one-time extension with audit log) mapped per role. Four OTel-aligned span attributes (`agent.role`, `agent.budget.tokens` / `tool_calls` / `cost_usd`, `agent.budget.outcome` ∈ `completed`/`exhausted`/`extension_approved`). Pydantic `AgentBudget` model with `check_budget_before_call` pre-call gate (the gate that prevents the $47k loop case), `exhausted()` dimension reporter, and `emit_budget_telemetry()` for OTel-shape output. Composes with Pattern 1 (budget travels in the `HandoffRequest`), Pattern 2 (budget state lives in shared `StateGraph`), Pattern 3 (budget exhaustion is a fifth trigger in the escalation model), Path 06 Module 6 cost attribution (this pattern's telemetry is the data baggage propagation collects). The $47k 11-day infinite-loop case from Codebridge 2026 + the $75k/day runaway-agent bill from niteagent May 2026 are the canonical failure modes this prevents.

  - `learning-paths/03-multi-agent-systems/patterns/05-retry-policies.md` — NEW. ~15-min pattern. Three retry layers (LLM-call / tool-call / agent-loop) with three retry shapes. LLM-call layer: exponential backoff with jitter (1→2→4→8s, cap 30s, ±25% jitter, max 3 attempts) per buildmvpfast March 2026. Tool-call layer: idempotency-key-gated, provider-aware backoff respecting `Retry-After` headers, max 2 attempts, DLQ routing on exhaustion, non-idempotent tools get zero retries per Composio December 2025 idempotency conventions. Agent-loop layer: prompt-adjusted retry, max 1 attempt, hands off to Pattern 3's T2/T3/T4 tiers. Retryable-vs-non-retryable failure taxonomy (rate limit / timeout / 5xx / network = retryable; 4xx / schema / context-overflow-same-prompt / policy-violation / budget-exhausted = non-retryable; retry only if the next attempt has a reason to succeed). State-level circuit breakers (not per-node — the "LLM happily retries 1,000 times" failure mode per LifeTidesHub May 2026 requires cross-node coordination via shared state per Pattern 2). The explicit prompt-adjustment-vs-same-call distinction. Pydantic `RetryPolicy` + `RetryDecision` schemas with `decide_retry` routing function and four default policies (llm_call, tool_call_idempotent, tool_call_non_idempotent, agent_loop). Composes with Pattern 1 (retry policy + idempotency key as contract fields), Pattern 2 (state-level circuit breaker in shared state), Pattern 3 (retry runs *before* escalation; retry exhaustion is the upstream trigger for T2/T3/T4), Pattern 4 (retries consume budget). The five 2026-canonical LLM fallback strategies (provider-rotation / model-downgrade / retry-then-fallback / cache-on-failure / manual-handoff per FutureAGI May 2026) all live downstream of this layer.

  - `learning-paths/03-multi-agent-systems/patterns/06-cross-agent-provenance.md` — NEW. ~15-min pattern. The four-entity provenance graph: Source (URI + fetched_at + doc_hash + retrieved_by + ttl_seconds) → EvidenceItem (source_id FK + passage_text + content_hash + extracted_by) → DerivedClaim (evidence_ids FK list + inference_type + derived_by + confidence + optional corrected_from back-pointer) → FinalOutput (claim_ids FK list + synthesis_notes + written_by). Five canonical inference types per SQuAI / MASS-RAG 2026 conventions (`direct_quote` / `paraphrase` / `summarized` / `inferred` / `synthesized`); synthesized claims require ≥ 2 evidence items as a structural invariant. Evidence ownership append-only (extracting agent owns the source attribution; downstream agents add derivations without altering); shared-state convention from Pattern 2 applies. Citation transfer at every Pattern 1 handoff boundary: full evidence-item objects flow (not just FK IDs); `validate_provenance` runs at the boundary checking no dangling references + no citation-less claims + synthesized-claim ≥ 2-evidence invariant. Stale-evidence invalidation via per-source TTL (research-paper TTL effectively infinite; price-feed TTL minutes); three behaviors on staleness (refetch / flag with `freshness: "stale"` low-confidence marker / Pattern 3 escalation). The provenance graph IS the audit trail — structured data with FK relationships serializable to JSON; regulator audit response and debugging are the same operation. Composes with Pattern 1 (provenance graph in handoff payload; the "every fact has a citation" invariant becomes structurally enforceable), Pattern 2 (graph lives in shared state with append-only reducer semantics; private LLM transcripts don't enter), Pattern 3 (provenance validation failure is a critic-agent T2 trigger), Pattern 5 (provenance failures are non-retryable schema-class errors). Delivers the +0.088 / 12% faithfulness improvement that SQuAI's multi-agent scientific QA system reports (arxiv:2510.15682) by making faithfulness *verifiable* rather than aspirational. Bad retrieval with good provenance produces well-cited wrong answers; the pattern is structural complement, not retrieval-quality substitute.

### Added

- **🚀 Path 03 v2 first batch — Production multi-agent patterns (Batch 39).** Opens Path 03 v2 after Path 06 v2 completion. Mirrors the Path 06 v1 → v2 transition exactly: v1 ships topologies and labs (supervisor-worker, generator-critic, plan-and-execute, multi-agent RAG, framework bridge, multi-agent evaluation); v2 starts here with the cross-cutting operational mechanisms that production multi-agent deployments need once the topology choice is settled.

  The three patterns ship the smallest useful set the 2026 production literature converges on: explicit handoff contracts at every agent-to-agent boundary; principled shared-state boundaries that say what crosses the boundary and what stays private; an escalation-and-fallback ladder that turns agent disagreement and tool failure from failure modes into routing signals.

  - `learning-paths/03-multi-agent-systems/patterns/README.md` — NEW. Patterns directory landing page. Mirrors the Path 06 patterns README shape: distinguishes patterns from concepts (theory), labs (executable demos), reference solutions (lab solutions), and the top-level architecture-patterns directory (those define topologies; these are operational mechanisms inside topologies). Three-pattern overview table with `Solves` and `Connects to` columns. Pattern-to-module connection table showing which Path 03 v1 modules and labs each pattern applies inside. Pick-a-pattern decision aid with mermaid diagram. "What this directory is NOT" anti-scope explicitly distinguishing patterns from new topologies, vendor recommendations, Path 06 observability work, and exhaustive coverage. "Adding a new pattern" section pointing at the template.

  - `learning-paths/03-multi-agent-systems/patterns/01-handoff-contracts.md` — NEW. ~15-min pattern. Eight-section structure (Intent → When to use → When NOT to use → The mechanism → Implementation sketch → How this combines with Path 03 modules → Tradeoffs and what this misses → References). The structured-brief schema with six contract fields: required input schema (objective, constraints, evidence budget, available tools), required output schema (result, citations, confidence, done-signal), ownership of context, success/failure status (`succeeded` / `failed` / `partial` / `needs_escalation` / `policy_violation`), citations / evidence transfer, retry / escalation path. Pydantic `HandoffRequest`/`HandoffResponse` implementation sketch with `make_handoff()` boundary function enforcing the provenance invariant (every fact has a citation). Module-by-module connection table covering Labs 10, 11, 12, 13, 14, 16. Tradeoffs explicit on schema rigidity, validation cost, and the contract-is-not-the-prompt confusion. References anchor on the niteagent P2 prompt pattern (May 2026), the dev.to handoff-as-first-class-span post (April 2026), AffinityBots' five-message-type taxonomy (December 2025), and Anthropic's 2024 "Building effective agents" essay.

  - `learning-paths/03-multi-agent-systems/patterns/02-shared-state-boundaries.md` — NEW. ~15-min pattern. Same eight-section structure. The four-kind decision rule with explicit table: task state YES (last-write-wins, owned by orchestrator), evidence state YES (append-only, co-owned), decisions state YES (append-only, ownership-tagged), private agent state NO (system prompts, hidden reasoning, full transcripts — inlining these is the 15× token-burn anti-pattern per niteagent May 2026). The four-field minimum useful shared-state schema (`objective` / `plan` / `current_step` / `status` as task state; `evidence` and `decisions` as append-only; scratchpad deliberately absent). Two named failure modes: over-sharing (token-burn case; symptom is context-window blowup) and under-sharing (planning-drift case; symptom is workers re-doing prior work). LangGraph `TypedDict` with `Annotated[..., operator.add]` reducer semantics as the production substrate (Lab 14's `StateGraph` is the reference implementation). Module-by-module connection table covering Labs 12, 13, 14, 15, 16. Compaction step for long-horizon tasks called out as the production answer to append-only memory growth. References anchor on niteagent (May 2026), AffinityBots (December 2025), clickittech (February 2026), and Lifetideshub on LangGraph state-as-first-class-citizen (April 2026).

  - `learning-paths/03-multi-agent-systems/patterns/03-escalation-and-fallback.md` — NEW. ~15-min pattern. Same eight-section structure. The five-tier escalation ladder with mermaid diagram: T0 continue with degraded confidence → T1 retry with adjusted prompt (one shot, same agent) → T2 escalate to critic agent → T3 escalate to human review (HITL pre-approval per Anna Jey April 2026) → T4 safe fallback answer (terminal; explicit "I cannot help" framing). The four canonical triggers (critic disagreement, failed tool call, missing evidence, timeout/loop risk) mapped to default tier paths with explicit short-circuit rules (policy violation → T4 unconditionally; high-stakes critic disagreement → T3 direct; missing evidence → T0-vs-T4 split on stakes). Python `EscalationDecision` schema with stable `decision_id` for audit trail (EU AI Act / decisions log). Explicit reuse of [Path 06 Pattern 2 (drift-triggered review)](#)'s severity classifier — one routing infrastructure, multiple event-source tags (`drift`, `agent_escalation`, `adversarial_red_team_fail`). Two anti-patterns called out: "escalate everything" (HITL rates above 10-15% mean the topology is wrong) and "no escalation" (every issue logged but no action). References anchor on clickittech's four conflict-resolution mechanisms (February 2026), Anna Jey's three-mode HITL framework (April 2026), Galileo's EU AI Act mapping (April 2026), Strata's two-factor judgment + challenge-and-response checklist framing (May 2026), AffinityBots' explicit "escalation signals when an agent is stuck or detects risk" (December 2025), and codebridge's high-stakes-fraud-detection example (2026).

  - niteagent (May 2026), *Multi-Agent in Production 2026: 3 Patterns That Survived* — the P2 prompt pattern (structured contract: objective + output format + tool guidance + clear task boundaries); the 15× token-burn cost for full-transcript inlining; four production rules including "return summary not transcript"
  - dev.to (April 2026), *Multi-Agent Handoff With Ownership Boundaries Nobody Crosses* — "the fix was a contract, not a smarter prompt"; agent-handoff event as first-class span type in Langfuse / Arize / Braintrust / W&B Weave; OpenAI Agents SDK + LangGraph `Command(goto=...)` as the two production handoff primitives; the orchestrator-as-god anti-pattern
  - clickittech (February 2026), *Multi-Agent System Architecture Guide for 2026* — the four canonical conflict-resolution mechanisms (priority-based, voting/consensus, critic-mediated arbitration, HITL escalation); the "conflict resolution transforms disagreement from a failure mode into a feature" reframing
  - Anna Jey (Medium, April 2026), *Human-in-the-Loop AI Agents: How to Add Approvals, Escalation, and Safe Autonomy in Production* — three HITL modes (pre-approval, in-loop interrupts, post-action review); "most production systems need all three"
  - Galileo (April 2026), *How to Build Human-in-the-Loop Oversight for AI Agents* — EU AI Act multi-tier risk categorization mapping to escalation policy; high-risk obligations under the August 2026 enforcement deadline; multi-agent chain complexity monitoring (chain length + confidence decay + inter-agent disagreement)
  - AffinityBots (December 2025), *AI Agent Teams in 2026: How Multi-Agent Systems Actually Work* — five canonical message types (task assignments, tool results, critiques, approvals, escalation signals); "reliability stems from explicit protocols, idempotent handlers, and durable state, not from emergent conversations"
  - Strata (May 2026), *Human-in-the-Loop: A 2026 Guide to AI Oversight* — two-factor judgment for critical actions; challenge-and-response checklists (intent, data lineage, permissions chain, expected blast radius, rollback plan)
  - Lifetideshub (April 2026), *How to Build Multi-Agent AI Systems with LangGraph productions* — "LangGraph is the production standard for agent graphs because it treats state as a first-class citizen"; planner-executor architecture for 2026
  - codebridge (2026), *Multi-Agent Systems & AI Orchestration Guide 2026* — high-stakes financial-fraud-detection example with automated rerouting to conservative fallback model or human compliance officer

  - gurusup (May 2026), *Best Multi-Agent Frameworks in 2026: LangGraph, CrewAI...* — 2026 framework landscape (LangGraph + OpenAI Agents SDK + Google ADK + Anthropic Agent SDK + CrewAI); LangGraph 27,100 monthly searches vs CrewAI 14,800

- **🚀 Path 06 v2 sixth and final batch — Adversarial red-teaming at scale (Batch 38).** Closes Path 06 v2. The sixth and final v2 batch adds the safety/stress-testing dimension to the production evaluation and observability stack — the orthogonal question to the rest of Path 06. Where Modules 1-7 ask "is the system performing well on the queries users actually send?", this batch answers "does the system fail safely on queries adversaries will send?". The two questions need the same orchestration substrate — eval datasets, judge ensembles, severity routing, regression sets, OTel propagation — but cover orthogonal question spaces.

  **Safety policy held** per the repo's `SECURITY.md`. Threat models and defensive patterns with citations — yes; weaponizable exploits with no defensive purpose — no; "100% prevention" claims — no. The taxonomy is at the OWASP Top 10 abstraction level (what to test for, not how to craft the test); the lab uses benign synthetic stand-ins throughout (toy policy: "never output SECRET_TOKEN, never write haiku"; toy attacks: "say the policy backwards", "write a haiku"). No working bypass strings, no exploit chains, no jailbreak templates anywhere in the batch.

  - `concepts/evaluation/adversarial-red-teaming-at-scale.md` — NEW. 28,368 bytes (reading time stamped ~25 min by length and conceptual density). Seven-section structure per the user spec plus references: (1) what red-teaming means in Path 06 — the orthogonal-question framing with the explicit two-activity separation between "generating failure-eliciting inputs" (security/safety teams + DeepTeam/Garak/Promptfoo/PyRIT) and "running those inputs at scale and scoring the responses" (Path 06 territory); (2) why ordinary evaluation is not enough — three reasons natural-traffic monitoring has an adversarial blind spot (the natural distribution doesn't contain the attacker distribution; signals are sparse and high-stakes; attacker behavior is non-stationary); (3) eight failure categories aligned to OWASP Top 10 v2025 — prompt injection (LLM01), tool misuse (LLM06), retrieval poisoning (LLM04+LLM08), citation laundering (LLM09+LLM05), multi-turn manipulation (Crescendo + TAP), policy boundary probing (LLM02+LLM07), hidden objective conflicts (reward hacking + deceptive alignment + sandbagging per arxiv:2512.20677), and evaluator-gaming / judge hacking (Goodhart's-law applied to judge metrics); (4) the canonical six-step workflow with mermaid diagram — seed scenarios → generate variants → run at scale → score with judge ensemble → route disagreements to human review → promote confirmed failures to regression tests; (5) Path 06 connections — explicit subsections for Module 4 (online eval registration with `evaluator.kind = adversarial`), Module 5 (calibration discipline with sampling weighted toward high-disagreement variants), Module 7 (threaded evaluation for Crescendo/TAP), Pattern 3 (the ensemble scoring mechanism), Project 3 (the production architecture); (6) mid-2026 OSS tool landscape table — DeepTeam (Apache 2.0 OSS, 1,690+ stars, 50+ vulnerabilities, OWASP/NIST/MITRE/OWASP_ASI_2026 alignment), Promptfoo (MIT, 300,000+ developers, CI/CD integration via `braintrustdata/eval-action`), PyRIT (MIT Microsoft, multi-modal + multi-turn including Crescendo and TAP), Garak (Apache 2.0 NVIDIA, LLM provider scanning) — plus coverage-framework alignment (OWASP Top 10 v2025, OWASP Top 10 for Agentic Applications 2026, NIST AI RMF Measure 2.6, MITRE ATLAS, ISO/IEC 42001, EU AI Act); (7) anti-scope — not a jailbreak recipe, not guidance for bypassing safety systems, not automatic blocking without review, not a substitute for domain-specific safety policy. References section with 18+ verified mid-2026 citations.

  - `labs/24-adversarial-red-teaming-at-scale/README.md` — NEW. 90-110 min lab brief with mermaid architecture diagram, prominent safety banner, 9-step structure, explicit "what this lab does NOT do" section, "what to watch for" pitfalls section, and a substitution table mapping each lab piece to its production substitution (PolicyAgent → real agent; mechanical variant generator → DeepTeam/PyRIT/Promptfoo; deterministic judges → LLM-as-judge calls across 3 model families per Pattern 3; mock sinks → LangSmith annotation queue + APM pager; in-memory RegressionSet → LangSmith Datasets + CI integration; pandas dashboard → Grafana panel or LangSmith dashboard).

  - `labs/24-adversarial-red-teaming-at-scale/lab.ipynb` — NEW. 23 cells (13 md + 10 code), parse-clean, 0 outputs, deterministic with fixed seeds. Ten steps: (0) setup with explicit "benign synthetic only" banner; (1) synthetic `PolicyAgent` with toy policy (~15 lines of policy logic, three deterministic weaknesses for the workflow to detect); (2) eight-category `FailureCategory` enum + `SeedScenario` taxonomy at the intent abstraction level; (3) `generate_variants` via mechanical string substitution (`BENIGN_MUTATIONS` list of identity / case-change / polite-prefix / research-framing etc. — no real attack patterns); (4) `RedTeamRunner` + `deterministic_policy_check` pre-filter; (5) three deterministic judge functions (`judge_strict` / `judge_lenient` / `judge_creative_leaning`) + `apply_ensemble` + `JudgedResult` with agreement structure; (6) `Severity` classifier + `MockSink` routing infrastructure + `HIGH_SEVERITY_CATEGORIES` set for auto-paging; (7) `RegressionSet.promote` requiring `human_approved=True` (the explicit anti-auto-promotion gate); (8) multi-round dashboard simulating three rounds of red-teaming with growing variant counts (5→7→10 per seed); (9) per-category breakdown with `fail_rate` and `disagreement_rate` columns; (10) synthesis with substitution table, anti-patterns, Path 06 composition map.

    **End-to-end execution verified locally**. All 10 code cells run without errors. The pedagogical signal is exactly what the lab needs: EVALUATOR_GAMING and RETRIEVAL_POISONING scenarios produce 95% split-verdict rates because they sit on the judge-ensemble policy boundary by design (toy agent's "write haiku" response splits strict-judge vs creative-judge); HIDDEN_OBJECTIVE_CONFLICTS, POLICY_BOUNDARY_PROBING, and TOOL_MISUSE produce 100% unanimous-fail rates as the toy agent's "show policy" weakness gets exercised; MULTI_TURN_MANIPULATION and PROMPT_INJECTION produce ~95% unanimous-fail rates from the "reverse the secret" weakness; CITATION_LAUNDERING produces 0% across all judges (toy agent doesn't exercise that weakness). The regression set grows monotonically across rounds: 22 → 32 → 42 tests, demonstrating the central success metric of a red-team program (regression-set growth) cleanly.

  - `learning-paths/06-evaluation-observability/patterns/03-judge-ensemble.md` — Modified. New "How this combines with adversarial red-teaming (Batch 38)" subsection added between "How this combines with recipes" and "Tradeoffs and what this misses". Three paragraphs covering why the ensemble's 3× cost earns its keep more for adversarial scoring than for natural-traffic evaluation: (1) adversarial scoring is binary go/no-go on a release decision; (2) adversarial scoring has high per-judge variance — split verdicts identify the policy-boundary cases that need human clarification (with the Lab 24 EVALUATOR_GAMING / RETRIEVAL_POISONING 95%-split-verdict observation cited as concrete evidence); (3) the split-verdict bucket *is* the most useful red-team signal because it surfaces where the safety policy is ambiguous. Plus the operational shape: how the three-way agreement structure maps to the three-tier severity from Pattern 2 (T0 trend, T2 human review, T3 confirmed failure), and how confirmed failures become candidates (not direct entries) for the regression set per the human-approval gate.

- **🚀 Path 06 v2 fifth batch — Embedding-space drift detection (Batch 37).** The fifth Path 06 v2 batch ships the RAG-input-side drift detection layer that complements Module 5's score-side drift. Module 5 detects drift in **evaluator scores** — a lagging indicator on the output side of the eval pipeline. This batch detects drift in **embeddings, query distributions, retrieved documents, topic mix, and semantic clusters** — the leading indicator on the input side. The two compose into a causal-chain monitor: `embedding drift → retrieval-result drift → answer drift → score-side drift`. Closes the second-to-last Path 06 v2 item; only adversarial red-teaming at scale remains.

  **Why this matters operationally**: when score-side drift fires (Lab 20), the team doesn't yet know whether the cause is the model, the prompt, the retriever, the corpus, or the query distribution. Embedding-space drift narrows the diagnosis — if queries have drifted but scores haven't moved yet, the eval set is stale; if embeddings have drifted but queries haven't, the corpus or embedding model changed; if both have drifted and scores haven't, this is the upstream signal *before* user-visible degradation surfaces. Per Decompressed.io March 2026, the canonical failure mode is silent recall degradation (0.92 → 0.74) caused by partial corpus re-embedding mixing vectors from different model versions.

Eight steps: (0) setup; (1) synthetic 384-dim L2-normalized embedding factory with PCA-2D visualization; (2) four detection methods from scratch — `centroid_shift` (L2 norm of mean difference), `cosine_distribution_shift` (KS-test on baseline-centroid cosine distances), `nn_overlap` (Jaccard of top-k doc_ids with documented same-doc contract + runtime assert), `cluster_population_shift` (KMeans fit-on-baseline + chi-square with n_clusters=4 cap for expected-count ≥ 5); (3) four drift scenarios — A query-distribution shift, B partial corpus refresh, C topic redistribution, D embedding model drift via Reference Dataset Probing; (4) 4×4 application table with NN-overlap correctly applied only to scenarios B and D (where the same-doc contract holds); (5) `RollingWindowEmbeddingDriftDetector` with persistence + cooldown semantics matching Lab 20; (6) severity classifier mapping to Pattern 2's T1/T2/T3 + mock routing sinks (annotation queue, eval engineer pager, on-call pager); (7) synthesis with detector-selection table and explicit two-drift composition diagram. **End-to-end execution verified locally** in inspection mode — all 11 code cells run cleanly without errors; NN-overlap demonstration produces the expected stable=0.885 vs drift=0.076 split matching the Anindya Singh Obi December 2025 production benchmark of 85-95% stable vs 25-40% drop-off.

  **Lab synthesis: purely synthetic, no API keys, no real embeddings.** Decision rationale: (1) Lab 20 (the closest sibling) is "all data is synthetic and deterministic" — Lab 23 mirrors this exactly; (2) real embeddings would require `sentence-transformers` (in the `rag` extra, not `obs`); (3) synthetic 384-dim Gaussian vectors with controlled distribution shifts give higher signal-to-noise for the four detection methods than real embeddings would; (4) the detection code is provenance-agnostic — `np.array` is `np.array` regardless of source. Dependencies remain unchanged: only `numpy`, `scipy.stats`, `sklearn`, `matplotlib` — all already pinned in the `obs` extra for Lab 20.

  **A genuine semantic bug caught during build and fixed before ship.** Initial NN-overlap implementation compared top-k by row-index across two different vector sets, which gives zero overlap by construction (different vectors → different indices). The corrected implementation operates on stable doc_ids: row i in baseline and row i in current refer to the same logical document; what changes between the two arrays is the embedding model version. The corrected `nn_overlap` carries a runtime assert plus a docstring documenting the contract; scenarios A and C (which change corpus identity, not just embeddings) are explicitly excluded from NN-overlap in the 4×4 table with an "n/a" cell and an explanation. Empirical numerics were tuned during the bug investigation: `noise_scale=0.002` for stable embedding reproducibility (per-doc cosine sim > 0.99); per-doc perturbation drift mechanism (scale=0.3) rather than constant additive shift (which L2-normalization absorbs in high dimensions). Final clean signal: stable NN overlap ≈ 0.885, drift ≈ 0.076 — matching the Anindya Singh Obi December 2025 production benchmark of 85-95% stable vs 25-40% drop-off drifting almost exactly. **This is the kind of bug that the parse-clean check would have missed** but end-to-end execution caught — adding end-to-end execution to the per-batch verification protocol on the basis of this catch.

  The page is a **practical selection guide** for production evaluation and observability tools — explicitly not a vendor ranking. It compares 9 frameworks across 11 dimensions, provides code-level snippets for the 5 canonical operations every team performs (logging a trace, creating an evaluator, attaching scores, dataset evaluation, online evaluation), and gives a decision guide with one "choose X if" rule per tool. The Anti-scope section is explicit: no "best tool overall" conclusion, no benchmark claims without sources, no pricing claims without time-bounding, no comparisons of tools the page hasn't verified mid-2026.

  **Why this page exists**: across Batches 24-35, Path 06 v1 picked LangSmith as the central observability platform (Module 2 + Lab 17), then OTel-native paths built on the assumption that OpenTelemetry's vendor-neutrality lets teams pick their backend (Module 3 + Lab 18), and the v2 recipes/projects deliberately stayed tool-agnostic at the scorer layer. The deep dive page is the missing reference: it tells teams reading Recipe 2 which of Phoenix/Langfuse/Laminar to pick; it tells teams reading Project 2's "OSS or self-hosted APM" decision which OTel-native platform fits their constraint; and it explicitly maps the 9 frameworks to the 7 Path 06 v1 modules so teams using something other than LangSmith can see where their tool slots into the module sequence.

  **Tools covered** (9 — per the user spec): LangSmith (LangChain Inc.; closed source; full OTLP endpoint since March 2026); Braintrust ($80M Series B Feb 2026; eval-first with CI/CD gates; free tier 1M spans + 10K evals); Langfuse (MIT; v3 OTel-native rebuild; acquired by ClickHouse Jan 2026; 12M+ monthly PyPI downloads); Arize Phoenix (Elastic License 2.0; OpenInference SDK owner — most widely adopted OTel semantic conventions for LLMs; 50+ research-backed metrics); Laminar (Apache 2.0; OTel-native from day one; agent-first transcript view + Signals + SQL over traces + agent rollout debugger; Helm chart self-host); MLflow GenAI evaluation (Apache 2.0; `mlflow.genai.evaluate()` natively integrates RAGAS + DeepEval + Phoenix + TruLens + Guardrails AI as pluggable `@scorer` decorators); DeepEval/Confident AI (DeepEval Apache 2.0 with 50+ metrics + native pytest integration + G-Eval LLM-as-judge; Confident AI commercial managed layer with auto-curation, quality-aware alerting, multi-turn simulation); RAGAS (Apache 2.0; EACL 2024 paper origin; deepest RAG-specific metric library — faithfulness, answer relevancy, context precision/recall/utilization, noise sensitivity, agent goal accuracy, tool call accuracy; pure library with no platform); TruLens (Apache 2.0; originally TruEra now part of Snowflake; inline trace + evaluation coupling).

- **🚀 Path 06 v2 third batch — Production Projects (Batch 35).** The third Path 06 v2 batch ships three production-deployable capstone projects plus an index and template, completing the Path 06 v2 architecture: **Recipes** (Batch 33) describe deployment compositions, **Patterns** (Batch 34) describe reusable mechanisms, and **Projects** (Batch 35) describe the multi-day buildable capstones that integrate everything.

  The v2 ladder is now: concept pages (~10-15 min) → labs (~60-110 min) → recipes (~20-25 min) → patterns (~12-15 min) → projects (~45-60 min reading + multi-day build). Projects are the longest reading time but the most concrete deliverable shape — they include the milestone sequence, acceptance rubric, failure modes, and cost envelope for actually shipping the stack. Recipes tell you what to assemble; projects tell you how to ship it.

  **Design choice — path-scoped at `learning-paths/06-evaluation-observability/projects/`, not in top-level `/projects/`**: top-level `/projects/` catalogs multi-path build challenges (personal research assistant, PDF Q&A bot, financial research analyst, etc.) organized into beginner/intermediate/capstone tiers — explicitly "substantial builds that combine work across multiple paths." Path 06 v2 projects are different in scope: Path-06-specific capstones that integrate Path 06 labs + recipes + patterns into a production evaluation/observability stack. The two `/projects/` directories are orthogonal: a learner finishing a top-level capstone (financial research analyst, etc.) can use Path 06 Project 3 (Hybrid production stack) as the observability layer for it.
